To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the rational function \( \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Degrees
- **Numerator Degree:** The highest power of \( x \) in the numerator is \( 2 \).
- **Denominator Degree:** The highest power of \( x \) in the denominator is also \( 2 \).

### Step 2: Leading Coefficients
- **Leading Coefficient of Numerator:** \( 3 \)
- **Leading Coefficient of Denominator:** \( 5 \)

### Step 3: Evaluate the Limit
For rational functions where the degrees of the numerator and denominator are equal, the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity is the ratio of their leading coefficients.

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{\text{Leading Coefficient of Numerator}}{\text{Leading Coefficient of Denominator}} = \frac{3}{5}
\]

Step-by-step explanation:

We are tasked with finding the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step 1: Identify the Form**

As \( x \) approaches 1, both the numerator and denominator approach zero:
- Numerator: \( (1)^3 - 1 = 0 \)
- Denominator: \( 1 - 1 = 0 \)

This results in an indeterminate form of \( \frac{0}{0} \).

**Step 2: Apply L'Hospital's Rule**

L'Hospital's Rule states that if the limit of a quotient of two functions is an indeterminate form, then it can be evaluated by taking the derivative of the numerator and the derivative of the denominator.

Differentiate both the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):
- Numerator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^3 - 1) = 3x^2 \)
- Denominator: \( \frac{d}{dx}(x - 1) = 1 \)

**Step 3: Compute the New Limit**

Now, compute the limit of the derivatives as \( x \) approaches 1:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{3x^2}{1} = 3(1)^2 = 3
\]

**Final Answer**
The limit is \(\boxed{3}\).

To determine the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} \), I can use L'Hospital's Rule because both the numerator and denominator approach 0, resulting in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

First, I'll differentiate both the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \). The derivative of the numerator \( 1 - \cos(x) \) is \( \sin(x) \), and the derivative of the denominator \( x^2 \) is \( 2x \). This gives me a new limit: \( \frac{\sin(x)}{2x} \).

Now, as \( x \) approaches 0, both the numerator \( \sin(x) \) and the denominator \( 2x \) still approach 0, leading to another indeterminate form. I'll apply L'Hospital's Rule a second time.

Differentiating the new numerator \( \sin(x) \) gives \( \cos(x) \), and differentiating the denominator \( 2x \) gives \( 2 \). This results in the limit: \( \frac{\cos(x)}{2} \).

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{{1 - \cos x}}{{x^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Indeterminate Form:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, both the numerator \( 1 - \cos x \) and the denominator \( x^2 \) approach 0.
   - This results in the indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \text{Numerator derivative: } \frac{d}{dx}(1 - \cos x) = \sin x
     \]
     \[
     \text{Denominator derivative: } \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x
     \]
   - The limit now becomes:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{{\sin x}}{{2x}}
     \]

3. **Check the New Limit:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, both \( \sin x \) and \( 2x \) approach 0, resulting in another indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).
   - Apply L'Hospital's Rule again.

4. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule a Second Time:**
   - Differentiate the numerator and the denominator once more:
     \[
     \text{Numerator derivative: } \frac{d}{dx}(\sin x) = \cos x
     \]
     \[
     \text{Denominator derivative: } \frac{d}{dx}(2x) = 2
     \]
   - The limit now becomes:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{{\cos x}}{2}
     \]

5. **Evaluate the Final Limit:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0, \( \cos(0) = 1 \).
   - Therefore, the limit is:
     \[
     \frac{1}{2}
     \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\frac{1}{2}}

To determine the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{x - \sin(x)}{x^3} \), I can use the Taylor series expansion of \( \sin(x) \). The Taylor series for \( \sin(x) \) around 0 is:

\[
\sin(x) = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)
\]

Substituting this into the expression, we get:

\[
x - \sin(x) = x - \left( x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3) \right) = \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)
\]

Now, dividing by \( x^3 \):

\[
\frac{\frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6} + o(1)
\]

As \( x \) approaches 0, the term \( o(1) \) approaches 0. Therefore, the limit is:

\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \sin(x)}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6}
\]
</think>

To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Use the Taylor Series Expansion for \(\sin x\):**
   
   The Taylor series expansion of \(\sin x\) around \(x = 0\) is:
   \[
   \sin x = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)
   \]
   
2. **Subtract the Series from \(x\):**
   
   Subtracting \(\sin x\) from \(x\):
   \[
   x - \sin x = x - \left( x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3) \right) = \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)
   \]
   
3. **Divide by \(x^3\):**
   
   Divide both sides of the equation by \(x^3\):
   \[
   \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} = \frac{\frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6} + o(1)
   \]
   
4. **Take the Limit as \(x\) Approaches 0:**
   
   As \(x\) approaches 0, the term \(o(1)\) (which represents higher-order terms that approach 0 faster than a constant) becomes negligible:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{1}{6} + o(1) \right) = \frac{1}{6}
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{6}}

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \), I can start by analyzing the highest degree terms in both the numerator and the denominator. Both have \( x^2 \) as their highest power.

Next, I'll factor out \( x^2 \) from both the numerator and the denominator to simplify the expression:

\[
\frac{x^2(1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}{x^2(1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}
\]

\[

\]

**Solution:**

We are to find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Degrees of the Polynomials:**
   - Both the numerator and the denominator are polynomials of degree 2.

2. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by the Highest Power of \( x \):**
   
   Since both the numerator and the denominator have \( x^2 \) as the highest power, we divide each term by \( x^2 \):
   \[
   \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \frac{\frac{x^2}{x^2} + \frac{x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{\frac{x^2}{x^2} - \frac{x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}
   \]

3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Simplifying each term:
   \[
   = \frac{1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}
   \]

4. **Evaluate the Limit as \( x \to \infty \):**
   
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach 0:
   \[
   = \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{1}
\]

To confirm this intuition, I can apply L’Hospital’s Rule since both the numerator and the denominator tend towards infinity as \( x \) approaches infinity. To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\ln(x)}{x}
\]

**Step 1: Understand the Behavior of Functions**

As \( x \) approaches infinity:
- The natural logarithm function, \( \ln(x) \), grows without bound but at a much slower rate compared to linear functions.
- The denominator \( x \) increases linearly.

**Step 2: Apply Limit Laws or L’Hospital’s Rule**

Since both the numerator and the denominator approach infinity as \( x \to \infty \), we can apply **L’Hospital’s Rule**, which states that if \( \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{\infty}{\infty} \) or \( \frac{0}{0} \), then:
\[
\lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
\]

**Step 3: Differentiate Numerator and Denominator**

- The derivative of the numerator \( \ln(x) \) is:
  \[
  \frac{d}{dx} \ln(x) = \frac{1}{x}
  \]
  
- The derivative of the denominator \( x \) is:
  \[
  \frac{d}{dx} x = 1
  \]

**Step 4: Apply L’Hospital’s Rule**

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\ln(x)}{x} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{1} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{1}{x}
\]

**Step 5: Evaluate the Simplified Limit**

As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( \frac{1}{x} \) approaches zero.

\boxed{0}

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of \( \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1} \), I can start by evaluating the function directly at \( x = 1 \). Both the numerator and denominator become zero, resulting in an indeterminate form. 

Since direct substitution leads to a 0/0 form, L'Hospital's Rule is applicable here. According to L'Hospital's Rule, if both the numerator and denominator of a limit are differentiable and approach zero or infinity as \( x \) approaches a certain point, then the limit can be found by taking the derivatives of the numerator and denominator separately.

First, I'll find the derivative of the numerator: \( (1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1 \). Using the chain rule, the derivative is:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left( (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \right) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}
\]

Next, the derivative of the denominator \( x - 1 \) is simply 1.

Now, applying L'Hospital's Rule:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}}{1}
\]

Evaluating this limit at \( x = 1 \):
\[
\frac{1^2 (1 + 1^3)^{-2/3}}{1} = 1 \times (2)^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}}

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Direct Substitution:**  
   First, let's try substituting \( x = 1 \) directly into the function.
   \[
   \frac{(1 + 1^3)^{1/3} - 1}{1 - 1} = \frac{(2)^{1/3} - 1}{0}
   \]
   This results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**  
   Since the limit is of the form \( \frac{0}{0} \), we can apply L'Hospital's Rule, which states that if \( \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), then:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
   \]
   
3. **Differentiate the Numerator and Denominator:**
   - **Numerator:** \( f(x) = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1 \)
     \[
     f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - \frac{d}{dx}(1) = \frac{1}{3}(1 + x^3)^{-2/3} \cdot 3x^2 = x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}
     \]
   - **Denominator:** \( g(x) = x - 1 \)
     \[
     g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) - \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 1
     \]

4. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x^3)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^2 (1 + x^3)^{-2/3}}{1}
   \]

5. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   Substitute \( x = 1 \) into the differentiated expression:
   \[
   \frac{1^2 (1 + 1^3)^{-2/3}}{1} = 1 \times (2)^{-2/3} = \frac{1}{2^{2/3}}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{2^{2/3}}}
\]

   \]
   
   This matches the general form \(\left(1 + \frac{k}{x}\right)^x\), where \(k = 2\).

2. **Apply the Known Limit:**
   
   Using the known result:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left(1 + \frac{k}{x}\right)^x = e^k
   \]
   
   Substituting \(k = 2\):
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)^x = e^2
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[

Simplifying the numerator: 

= [cos²(x) + sin²(x)] / cos²(x)

The derivative of the function \( f(x) = \tan(x) \) can be found using differentiation rules for trigonometric functions. 

1. **Express \( \tan(x) \)** as \( \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)} \).
2. **Apply the quotient rule**: If \( h(x) = \frac{g(x)}{f(x)} \), then 
   \[
   h'(x) = \frac{g'(x)f(x) - g(x)f'(x)}{[f(x)]^2}
   \]
3. Here, \( g(x) = \sin(x) \) and \( f(x) = \cos(x) \).
4. Compute derivatives:
   - \( g'(x) = \cos(x) \)
   - \( f'(x) = -\sin(x) \)
5. Substitute into the quotient rule formula:
   \[
   h'(x) = \frac{\cos(x)\cos(x) - (-\sin(x))\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]
6. Simplify numerator using \( \cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x) = 1 \):
   \[
   h'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]
7. Recognize that \( \frac{1}{\cos(x)} = \sec(x) \), so:
   \[
   h'(x) = \sec^2(x)
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( f(x) = \tan(x) \) is \( \sec^2(x) \).

The function \( f(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is differentiated using the product rule, resulting in:
\[
f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Identify the Components**: Recognize that \( f(x) \) is a product of two functions: \( u(x) = \cos x \) and \( v(x) = \ln x \).

2. **Apply the Product Rule**: The derivative of \( f(x) = u(x)v(x) \) is given by:
   \[
   f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
   \]

3. **Differentiate Each Part**:
   - \( u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (\cos x) = -\sin x \)
   - \( v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

4. **Substitute into the Product Rule Formula**:
   \[
   f'(x) = (-\sin x)(\ln x) + (\cos x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
   \]

5. **Simplify**:
   \[
   f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
   \]

**Final Answer**: 
The derivative of \( f(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is \( f'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x} \).

To find the derivative of \( x^x \), we can use logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
   
2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides:**
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
   
3. **Use logarithm properties to simplify the right side:**
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
   
4. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**
   - Left side:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - Right side (using the product rule):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [x \cdot \ln(x)] = \ln(x) + 1
     \]
     
5. **Set the derivatives equal to each other:**
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
   
6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
   
7. **Substitute \( y = x^x \) back into the equation:**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\(\boxed{x^{x} (1 + \ln x)}\)

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use the chain rule.

**Step 1:** Rewrite the function using a negative exponent for easier differentiation:
\[
f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
\]

**Step 2:** Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \). Then, \( f(u) = u^{-1} \).

**Step 3:** Differentiate the outer function with respect to \( u \):
\[
\frac{df}{du} = -u^{-2}
\]

**Step 4:** Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \):
\[
\frac{du}{dx} = 2x
\]

**Step 5:** Apply the chain rule by multiplying the derivatives:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{df}{du} \times \frac{du}{dx} = -u^{-2} \times 2x = -2x(x^2 + 1)^{-2}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
f'(x) = \boxed{\dfrac{-2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = e^x \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = 1 + e^x
   \]

2. **Compute the derivatives \( u'(x) \) and \( v'(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = e^x \quad \text{and} \quad v'(x) = e^x
   \]

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
   \]
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the Numerator:**
   \[
   e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x) = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x
   \]
   
5. **Final Simplified Derivative:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\dfrac{e^{x}}{\left(e^{x}+1\right)^{2}}}

The derivative of arctan(x) with respect to x is calculated using implicit differentiation and trigonometric identities.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. Let \( y = \arctan(x) \). This implies that \( \tan(y) = x \).
2. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: The derivative of \( \tan(y) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \) (using the chain rule).
   - Right side: The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.
3. Set up the equation:
   \[
   \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
   \]
4. Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
   \]
5. Use the trigonometric identity \( \tan^2(y) + 1 = \sec^2(y) \). Since \( \tan(y) = x \), substitute to get:
   \[
   \sec^2(y) = x^2 + 1
   \]
6. Substitute back into the derivative expression:
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( \arctan(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \).

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let:
\[
u(x) = x \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = \ln x
\]

**Step 2: Find the Derivatives of Each Component**

First, find \( u'(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
\]

Next, find \( v'(x) \):
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Product Rule**

Substitute these derivatives back into the Product Rule formula:
\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
f'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression**

Simplify each term:
\[
f'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

\[
\boxed{\ln x + 1}

To find the derivative of the function \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \(f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)}\), then its derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
\]

   \[
   g(x) = \sin x \\
   h(x) = x^2 + 1
   \]

   \[
   g'(x) = \cos x \\
   h'(x) = 2x
   \]

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression if possible:**
   In this case, there's no further simplification required.

The chain rule is where if you have a function inside another function, you take the derivative of the outer function and multiply it by the derivative of the inner function.

So, F(x) = e^{x^2}. Let me denote u = x^2, which makes F(x) = e^u. Now, applying the chain rule, the first derivative F'(x) should be e^u times the derivative of u with respect to x. The derivative of x squared is 2x, so putting it together, F'(x) = e^{x^2} * 2x.

Okay, that gives me the first derivative: F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2}. Now, I need the second derivative, which means taking the derivative of F'(x). So, let's apply differentiation again to F'(x).

Looking at F'(x), it's a product of two functions: 2x and e^{x^2}. That means I should use the product rule here. The product rule states that if you have two functions multiplied together, their derivative is the derivative of the first times the second plus the first times the derivative of the second.

Let me set g(x) = 2x and h(x) = e^{x^2}. Then F'(x) = g(x) * h(x). Applying the product rule, F''(x) = g'(x)*h(x) + g(x)*h'(x).

First, find g'(x): The derivative of 2x is just 2.

Next, find h'(x), which is the derivative of e^{x^2}. Again, using the chain rule. Let u = x^2, so h(x) = e^u. Then h'(x) = e^u * du/dx = e^{x^2} * 2x.

Putting it all back into the product rule formula: F''(x) = 2 * e^{x^2} + 2x * (e^{x^2} * 2x). Simplifying that, I get:

F''(x) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x² e^{x^2}.

I can factor out the common term of e^{x^2}, so it becomes F''(x) = e^{x^2}(2 + 4x²). Alternatively, I could write this as F''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x²).

- First derivative: d/dx [e^{x^2}] = 2x e^{x^2}.
- Second derivative requires differentiating that result. So, the product rule for 2x and e^{x^2} gives (2) * e^{x^2} + 2x * (2x e^{x^2}) which is indeed 2e^{x^2} + 4x² e^{x^2}. Factoring out 2e^{x^2} gives 2(1 + 2x²)e^{x^2}, so that's correct.

The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is found by first taking the first derivative using the chain rule and then applying the product rule to find the second derivative. 

Step-by-step explanation:
1. Let \( u = x^2 \), so \( F(x) = e^u \).
2. First derivative: \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \).
3. Second derivative using product rule on \( F'(x) \):
   - \( g(x) = 2x \) with \( g'(x) = 2 \)
   - \( h(x) = e^{x^2} \) with \( h'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \)
4. Apply product rule: 
   - \( F''(x) = (2)(e^{x^2}) + (2x)(2x e^{x^2}) = 2e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2} = 2(1 + 2x^2)e^{x^2} \).

   The given expression has both numerator and denominator as polynomials of degree 2.

2. **Determine the Leading Coefficients**:  
   - Numerator's leading coefficient: 3
   - Denominator's leading coefficient: 5

3. **Calculate the Limit**:  
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms with the highest degree dominate. Therefore:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{\text{Leading Coefficient of Numerator}}{\text{Leading Coefficient of Denominator}} = \frac{3}{5}
   \]

First, substituting \( x = 1 \) directly into the function results in a division by zero, which is undefined. This means I can't simply evaluate it at that point.

Next, I notice that both the numerator and denominator approach zero as \( x \) approaches 1, indicating that this is an indeterminate form of type \( \frac{0}{0} \).

To resolve this, I'll factor the numerator using the difference of cubes formula: \( a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2) \). Applying this to \( x^3 - 1 \), it becomes \( (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1) \).

Now, the function simplifies to \( \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} \). I can cancel out the \( x - 1 \) terms in both the numerator and denominator.

Finally, taking the limit as \( x \) approaches 1, I substitute 1 into this simplified expression: \( 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3 \).

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Substitute \( x = 1 \) directly into the function:**  
   Plugging in \( x = 1 \), we get:
   \[
   \frac{1^3 - 1}{1 - 1} = \frac{0}{0}
   \]
   This results in an indeterminate form (\( \frac{0}{0} \)), so we need to simplify the expression further.

2. **Factor the numerator:**  
   The numerator \( x^3 - 1 \) is a difference of cubes, which can be factored using the formula:
   \[
   a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2)
   \]
   Applying this to \( x^3 - 1 \):
   \[
   x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
   \]

3. **Simplify the expression:**  
   Substitute the factored form of the numerator back into the original fraction:
   \[
   \frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
   \]
   Notice that \( (x - 1) \) is a common factor in both the numerator and the denominator. Provided \( x \neq 1 \), we can cancel these terms:
   \[
   x^2 + x + 1
   \]

4. **Compute the limit:**  
   Now, take the limit as \( x \) approaches 1 of the simplified expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 1}} (x^2 + x + 1)
   \]
   Substitute \( x = 1 \):
   \[
   1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{3}
\]

I need to find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} \).

First, I'll consider substituting \( x = 0 \) directly into the function. The numerator becomes \( \ln(1+0) = \ln(1) = 0 \), and the denominator is also 0. This results in an indeterminate form of \( \frac{0}{0} \).

Since we have an indeterminate form, I can apply L'Hospital's Rule, which states that if both the numerator and denominator approach 0 or infinity as \( x \) approaches a certain value, then the limit of their ratio is equal to the limit of the ratio of their derivatives.

Next, I'll find the derivatives of the numerator and the denominator. The derivative of the numerator, \( \ln(1+x) \), with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{1+x} \). The derivative of the denominator, \( x \), with respect to \( x \) is 1.

Now, I'll take the ratio of these derivatives: \( \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \frac{1}{1+x} \).

Finally, I'll evaluate this expression as \( x \) approaches 0. Substituting \( x = 0 \), we get \( \frac{1}{1+0} = 1 \).

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x}
\]
we can use **L'Hospital's Rule** because directly substituting \( x = 0 \) results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the Indeterminate Form:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \ln(1 + x) = \ln(1) = 0
   \]
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} x = 0
   \]
   So, we have \( \frac{0}{0} \), which is an indeterminate form.

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \).
   
   - **Numerator's Derivative:**
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(1 + x)] = \frac{1}{1 + x}
     \]
     
   - **Denominator's Derivative:**
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} [x] = 1
     \]
   
3. **Compute the New Limit:**
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1 + x}}{1} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{1 + x}
   \]

4. **Evaluate at \( x = 0 \):**
   \[
   \frac{1}{1 + 0} = 1
   \]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{1}
\]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the function \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \), I can start by dividing both the numerator and the denominator by the highest power of \( x \) present, which is \( x^2 \).

This simplifies the expression to:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}
\]

As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach zero. Therefore, the expression simplifies to:

\[
\frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
\]
</think>

To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Highest Power of \( x \):**
   The highest power of \( x \) in both the numerator and the denominator is \( x^2 \).

2. **Divide Each Term by \( x^2 \):**
   Divide every term in both the numerator and the denominator by \( x^2 \):
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\frac{x^2}{x^2} + \frac{x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{\frac{x^2}{x^2} - \frac{x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}
   \]
   
3. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Simplify each term:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}
   \]
   
4. **Evaluate the Limit as \( x \) Approaches Infinity:**
   As \( x \to \infty \), the terms \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach 0:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
   \]

\[
\boxed{1}

To determine the limit 

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{( (1 + x)^3 )^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1}
\]

we first simplify the expression inside the limit. The function \( f(x) = ((1 + x)^3)^{1/3} \) simplifies to \( 1 + x \) because raising a number to the third power and then taking the cube root (considering real numbers) returns the original value. Therefore, we have:

\[
f(x) = (1 + x)
\]

Substituting this back into the limit expression, we get:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(1 + x - 1)}{x - 1}
\]

Simplifying the numerator \( (1 + x - 1) \) gives us \( x \). Thus, the limit becomes:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x}{x - 1}
\]

To evaluate this limit, we consider the behavior as \( x \) approaches 1 from both sides. 

- As \( x \) approaches 1 from the right (i.e., \( x \to 1^+ \)), the denominator \( x - 1 \) approaches 0 from the positive side, making the entire expression approach \( +\infty \).
- As \( x \) approaches 1 from the left (i.e., \( x \to 1^- \)), the denominator \( x - 1 \) approaches 0 from the negative side, making the entire expression approach \( -\infty \).

Since the one-sided limits are not equal and tend to opposite infinities, the overall limit does not exist.

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{\text{DNE}}
\]

The problem requires finding the derivative of the quadratic function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \).

To solve this, we'll use basic differentiation rules:

1. **Power Rule**: The derivative of \( x^n \) with respect to \( x \) is \( nx^{n-1} \).
2. **Sum/Difference Rule**: The derivative of a sum or difference of functions is the sum or difference of their derivatives.

Applying these rules step by step:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) - \frac{d}{dx}(5x) + \frac{d}{dx}(4)
\]

Calculate each term separately:

- \( \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(5x) = 5 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0 \)

Combining these results:

\[
f'(x) = 6x - 5
\]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of the function is:
\[
\boxed{6x - 5}
\]

   The given function is:
   \[
   f(x) = \ln(x)
   \]

   \[

   \]

   \]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \), we will apply the **power rule** to each term. The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

Let's differentiate each term separately:

1. **Differentiate \( x^3 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^{2}
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( 2x^2 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(2x^2) = 4x
   \]

3. **Differentiate \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
   \]

4. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0
   \]

Now, combine all the derivatives:
\[
f'(x) = 3x^{2} + 4x + 1 + 0 = 3x^{2} + 4x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{3x^2 + 4x + 1}
\]

\[

\]

\[
F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) - \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) + \frac{d}{dx}(1)

\[
F''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x

The derivative of \( f(x) = \tan(x) \) is found using the quotient rule since \( \tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)} \). Applying the quotient rule:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{\cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x)}{\cos^2(x)} = \frac{1}{\cos^2(x)} = \sec^2(x)
\]

Final Answer: "The derivative of tan x is sec²x"

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if a function is the product of two functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = e^x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = e^x \quad \text{(since the derivative of } e^x \text{ is } e^x\text{)}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
\]
\[
f'(x) = e^x + x e^x
\]
\[
f'(x) = (1 + x)e^x

- The derivative of \( \sin(x) \) is \( \cos(x) \).

**Solution:**

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll proceed step by step.

1. **First Derivative (\( F'(x) \)):**
   
   We'll use the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( f(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then
   \[
   f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
   \]
   
   Here:
   - Let \( u(x) = x^2 \) ⇒ \( u'(x) = 2x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = e^x \) ⇒ \( v'(x) = e^x \)
   
   Applying the product rule:
   \[
   F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
   \]

2. **Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \)):**
   
   Now, we'll differentiate \( F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x \).

   - Differentiate the first term \( 2x e^x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(2x e^x) = 2e^x + 2x e^x
     \]
     
   - Differentiate the second term \( x^2 e^x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 e^x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
     \]
     
   Adding both derivatives together:
   \[
   F''(x) = 2e^x + 2x e^x + 2x e^x + x^2 e^x = 2e^x + 4x e^x + x^2 e^x
   \]
   
3. **Final Answer:**
   
   Combining the terms:
   \[
   F''(x) = (x^2 + 4x + 2)e^x
   \]

**Answer:**

\boxed{(x^2 + 4x + 2)e^{x}}

I need to find the derivative of the function f(x) = 1 / (x² + 1). To do this, I'll use the chain rule. 

First, rewrite f(x) as f(x) = (x² + 1)^(-1).

Let’s set u = x² + 1, so f(u) = u^(-1).

Now, find the derivative of f with respect to u: df/du = -u^(-2).

Next, find the derivative of u with respect to x: du/dx = 2x.

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use the **chain rule**. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

1. **Rewrite the function**:  
   Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \). Then, \( f(u) = \frac{1}{u} = u^{-1} \).

2. **Differentiate with respect to \( u \)**:  
   \[
   \frac{df}{du} = -u^{-2}
   \]

3. **Differentiate \( u \) with respect to \( x \)**:  
   \[
   \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
   \]

4. **Apply the chain rule**:  
   Multiply the derivatives together:
   \[
   \frac{df}{dx} = \frac{df}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = (-u^{-2}) \cdot (2x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \) is:

\[
f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

The derivative of f(x) = \(\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\) is calculated using the quotient rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify u and v:**
   - Let \( u = \ln x \)
   - Let \( v = x^2 \)

2. **Compute the derivatives of u and v:**
   - \( u' = \frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
   - \( v' = \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x \)

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   The quotient rule states that if \( f(x) = \frac{u}{v} \), then
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
   \]
   
4. **Substitute u, v, u', and v' into the formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
   \]

5. **Simplify the numerator:**
   \[
   \left(\frac{1}{x}\right)(x^2) = x
   \]
   So, the numerator becomes:
   \[
   x - 2x \ln x
   \]

6. **Simplify the denominator:**
   \[
   (x^2)^2 = x^4
   \]

7. **Factor out x from the numerator:**
   \[
   x(1 - 2\ln x)
   \]
   
8. **Combine and simplify the expression:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2\ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\(\boxed{\text{Final Answer: }\dfrac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^{3}}}\)

   - Outer function: Natural logarithm, \( \ln(u) \)
   - Inner function: \( u = \sin x \)

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
h'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's identify the numerator and denominator of our function:
- \( u(x) = e^x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \)

First, find their derivatives:
- \( u'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) is itself)
- \( v'(x) = e^x \) (again, because the derivative of \( e^x \) is \( e^x \))

Now, apply the quotient rule:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:

\[
e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x) = e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2 = e^x
\]

So, the derivative simplifies to:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \(\sec(x)\) with respect to \(x\) can be found using known differentiation rules or trigonometric identities. Here's the step-by-step explanation:

1. **Express secant in terms of cosine**:
   \[
   \sec(x) = \frac{1}{\cos(x)}
   \]

2. **Apply the quotient rule for differentiation**: The derivative of \(f(x)/g(x)\) is \(\frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}\). Here, \(f(x) = 1\) and \(g(x) = \cos(x)\).

3. **Differentiate the numerator (f(x))**:
   \[
   f'(x) = 0
   \]

4. **Differentiate the denominator (g(x))**:
   \[
   g'(x) = -\sin(x)
   \]

5. **Substitute into the quotient rule formula**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(\sec(x)) = \frac{0 \cdot \cos(x) - 1 \cdot (-\sin(x))}{\cos^2(x)} = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]

6. **Simplify using trigonometric identity**:
   Recognize that \(\frac{\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)}\) can be rewritten as \(\sec(x)\tan(x)\) since \(\tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}\).

**Final Answer**: {Final Answer: "sec(x) tan(x)"}

The limit of (sin x)/x as x approaches 0 is a classic problem in calculus.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Problem Statement:** Find the limit as x approaches 0 of sin(x) divided by x, i.e., limₓ→₀ [sin(x)/x].

2. **Direct Substitution:** If we try substituting x = 0 directly, both the numerator (sin(0)) and denominator (0) become zero, resulting in an indeterminate form 0/0.

3. **Using L'Hospital's Rule:** Since we have an indeterminate form of type 0/0, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule which states that if lim f(x)/g(x) is an indeterminate form 0/0 or ∞/∞, then the limit can be found by taking the derivatives of f(x) and g(x):

   \[
   \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
   \]
   
4. **Differentiating Numerator and Denominator:**
   - The derivative of sin(x) with respect to x is cos(x).
   - The derivative of x with respect to x is 1.

5. **Applying L'Hospital's Rule:**

   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\cos(x)}{1}
   \]

6. **Evaluating the New Limit:**

   As x approaches 0, cos(0) equals 1.

7. **Final Result:** Therefore,

   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1
   \]

**Answer:** {Final Answer: "1"}

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the rational function \( \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} \) can be found by focusing on the leading terms in both the numerator and the denominator.

When evaluating such limits, the highest degree terms dominate because they grow faster as \( x \) becomes very large. Here, both the numerator and the denominator are polynomials of degree 2.

Dividing each term by \( x^2 \), we get:
\[
\frac{3 + \frac{2}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{5 + \frac{4}{x} + \frac{2}{x^2}}
\]
As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms with \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach zero. Thus, the limit simplifies to:
\[
\frac{3}{5}
\]

The limit is \(\boxed{\dfrac{3}{5}}\).

To find the limit of \(\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}\) as \(x\) approaches 1, we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Problem
We need to evaluate:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}
\]

### Step 2: Direct Substitution
If we try to substitute \(x = 1\) directly, both the numerator and denominator become zero:
\[
\frac{1^3 - 1}{1 - 1} = \frac{0}{0}
\]
This is an indeterminate form, so we need another method.

### Step 3: Factor the Numerator
Notice that \(x^3 - 1\) can be factored using the difference of cubes formula:
\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]
So, the expression becomes:
\[
\frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}
\]

### Step 4: Simplify the Expression
We can cancel out the \((x - 1)\) terms (as long as \(x \neq 1\)):
\[
\frac{\cancel{(x - 1)}(x^2 + x + 1)}{\cancel{x - 1}} = x^2 + x + 1
\]

### Step 5: Take the Limit
Now, substitute \(x = 1\) into the simplified expression:
\[
\lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
\]

### Final Answer

```json
{Final Answer: "3"}
```

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Taylor Series Expansion Approach:**
   - Expand cos x around x = 0:
     \[ \cos x = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2!} + \frac{x^4}{4!} - \cdots \]
   - Subtract from 1 and simplify for small x (neglecting higher-order terms):
     \[ 1 - \cos x \approx \frac{x^2}{2} \]
   - Substitute into the original limit:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\frac{x^2}{2}}{x^2} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2}
     \]

   - Original expression:
     \[

   - Both numerator and denominator approach 0, so apply L'Hospital's Rule by differentiating numerator and denominator once:
     \[

     \[

   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]

   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} x \cdot \ln(x)

To determine the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2^x}{x^3}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understanding the Functions Involved:**
   - The numerator is an exponential function \( 2^x \).
   - The denominator is a polynomial function \( x^3 \).

2. **Comparing Growth Rates:**
   - Exponential functions grow much faster than polynomial functions as \( x \) approaches infinity.
   - Therefore, \( 2^x \) will dominate \( x^3 \) in the long run.

3. **Evaluating the Limit:**
   - As \( x \to \infty \), \( 2^x \) increases without bound.
   - The denominator \( x^3 \) also increases but at a much slower rate compared to \( 2^x \).
   - This results in the fraction \( \frac{2^x}{x^3} \) growing without bound.

4. **Conclusion:**
   - Hence, the limit of \( \frac{2^x}{x^3} \) as \( x \) approaches infinity is infinity.

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\infty}
\]

To evaluate the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Form of the Limit:**
   - As \( x \) approaches \( 0^+ \), both the numerator \( \ln(1+x) \) and the denominator \( x \) approach 0.
   - This results in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - L'Hospital's Rule states that if \( \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \) results in an indeterminate form of \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), then:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
     \]
   - Differentiate the numerator and the denominator with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     f(x) = \ln(1+x) \quad \Rightarrow \quad f'(x) = \frac{1}{1+x}
     \]
     \[
     g(x) = x \quad \Rightarrow \quad g'(x) = 1
     \]

3. **Compute the New Limit:**
   - Substitute the derivatives into L'Hospital's Rule:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1} = \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{1}{1+x}
     \]
   - Evaluate the limit as \( x \) approaches \( 0^+ \):
     \[
     \frac{1}{1+0} = 1
     \]

\boxed{1}

   When \( x = 1 \), both the numerator and denominator become 0, resulting in an indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

   Let’s set \( y = (1 + x^3)^{1/3} \). Then, as \( x \to 1 \), \( y \to 2 \) because:
   \[

   \]

   The expression becomes:
   \[

   \]

   \]

   \[
   x^3 = (y^3 - 1)
   \]

   \[

- The derivative of \( \ln(x) \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \).

To find the derivative of \( x^x \), we can use logarithmic differentiation. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

**Step 1: Let \( y = x^x \)**

**Step 2: Take the natural logarithm of both sides**
\[
\ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
\]
Using the property \( \ln(a^b) = b \cdot \ln(a) \):
\[
\ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

**Step 3: Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \)**
\[
\frac{d}{dx} [\ln(y)] = \frac{d}{dx} [x \cdot \ln(x)]
\]
Using the chain rule on the left side:
\[
\frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx} [x] \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(x)]
\]

**Step 4: Compute the derivatives**
\[
\frac{d}{dx} [x] = 1
\]
\[
\frac{d}{dx} [\ln(x)] = \frac{1}{x}
\]
So,
\[
\frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]
Simplify:
\[
\frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
\]

**Step 5: Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \)**
Multiply both sides by \( y \):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
\]
Substitute back \( y = x^x \):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{x^{x}(\ln(x) + 1)}
\]

To find the second derivative of the function \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we will use the product rule twice. Let's proceed step by step.

### First Derivative:
Let \( u(x) = x^2 \) and \( v(x) = e^x \).

1. Compute the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \):
   \[
   u'(x) = 2x
   \]
   \[
   v'(x) = e^x
   \]

2. Apply the product rule:
   \[
   F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
   \]
   \[
   F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
   \]

### Second Derivative:
Now, we'll find the derivative of \( F'(x) \).

1. Rewrite \( F'(x) \):
   \[
   F'(x) = (2x)e^x + (x^2)e^x
   \]

2. Apply the product rule to each term separately.

**For the first term \( 2x e^x \):**
   - Let \( u_1(x) = 2x \) and \( v_1(x) = e^x \).
   - Compute their derivatives:
     \[
     u_1'(x) = 2
     \]
     \[
     v_1'(x) = e^x
     \]
   - Apply the product rule:
     \[
     (2x e^x)' = 2e^x + 2x e^x
     \]

**For the second term \( x^2 e^x \):**
   - Let \( u_2(x) = x^2 \) and \( v_2(x) = e^x \).
   - Compute their derivatives:
     \[
     u_2'(x) = 2x
     \]
     \[
     v_2'(x) = e^x
     \]
   - Apply the product rule:
     \[
     (x^2 e^x)' = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
     \]

3. Combine the results from both terms:
   \[
   F''(x) = (2e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x^2 e^x)
   \]

   \[

**Solution:**

We are given the function:
\[ f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) \), we'll use the **chain rule** and **power rule**.

1. **Rewrite the Function:**
   \[ f(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \]

2. **Apply the Power Rule:**
   The power rule states that if \( f(x) = g(x)^n \), then:
   \[ f'(x) = n \cdot g(x)^{n-1} \cdot g'(x) \]
   
   Here, \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \) and \( n = -1 \).

3. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**
   \[ g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^2 + 1) = 2x \]

4. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   Substitute back into the derivative:
   \[
   f'(x) = (-1) \cdot (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x
   \]
   
5. **Simplify the Expression:**
   \[ f'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \]

**Final Answer:**
\[ \boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^2+1)^2}} \]

The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \) is calculated using the quotient rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Functions:** Let \( u = \ln x \) and \( v = x^2 \).
   
2. **Compute Derivatives:**
   - \( u' = \frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
   - \( v' = \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x \)

3. **Apply Quotient Rule:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
   \]
   
4. **Substitute Values:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
   \]

5. **Simplify Numerator and Denominator:**
   - Numerator: \( \frac{x^2}{x} - 2x \ln x = x - 2x \ln x \)
   - Denominator: \( x^4 \)

6. **Final Simplification:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2\ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3}
   \]

**Answer:**  
The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \) is:
\[ f'(x) = \boxed{\dfrac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^{3}}} \]

**Solution:**

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the **chain rule**. The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( h(x) = g(j(x)) \), then its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = g'(j(x)) \cdot j'(x)
\]

In this case:
- The outer function \( g(u) = \ln(u) \)
- The inner function \( j(x) = \sin x \)

First, find the derivative of the outer function with respect to \( u \):

\[
g'(u) = \frac{d}{du} \ln(u) = \frac{1}{u}
\]

Next, find the derivative of the inner function with respect to \( x \):

\[
j'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \sin x = \cos x
\]

Now, apply the chain rule:

\[
f'(x) = g'(j(x)) \cdot j'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x = \cot x
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\cot x}
\]

The function \( f(x) = x \ln x \) can be differentiated using the product rule, resulting in:

\[
f'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Components:** Recognize that \( f(x) \) is a product of two functions: \( u(x) = x \) and \( v(x) = \ln x \).

2. **Apply the Product Rule:** The derivative of a product is given by:
   \[
   (u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v'
   \]
   
3. **Compute Derivatives:**
   - \( u'(x) = 1 \) since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.
   - \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) because the derivative of \( \ln x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \).

4. **Substitute into the Product Rule Formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = (1)(\ln x) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
   \]

5. **Simplify the Expression:**
   - \( (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = 1 \)

6. **Final Result:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \ln x + 1
   \]

**Answer:** {Final Answer: "ln(x) + 1"}

Just to make sure I didn't make any mistakes in the algebra:

- First term: derivative of 2x is 2.
- Second term: applying chain rule on e^{x²} gives 2x e^{x²}, multiplied by the original u(x) which is 2x, so that's 4x² e^{x²}.
Adding both terms: 2 e^{x²} + 4x² e^{x²} = (2 + 4x²)e^{x²}. 

Alternatively, factor out a 2 to get 2(1 + 2x²) e^{x²}, which is the same thing.

So yeah, that seems right. I think this is my final answer for the second derivative of F(x).
</think>

The function F(x) = e^{x^2} has its first derivative as F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2}. Applying the product rule to find the second derivative:

F''(x) = d/dx [2x] * e^{x^2} + 2x * d/dx [e^{x^2}]
       = 2 e^{x^2} + 2x*(2x e^{x^2})
       = 2 e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
       = (4x^2 + 2) e^{x^2}

**Final Answer:** The second derivative of F(x) is (4x² + 2)e^{x²}.

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), I'll start by recognizing that this is a quotient of two functions. Let’s denote the numerator as \( u = x \) and the denominator as \( v = 1 + x^2 \).

Next, I need to find the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \). The derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \) is straightforward: \( u' = 1 \). For \( v \), applying the power rule gives \( v' = 2x \).

Now, using the quotient rule formula:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{v \cdot u' - u \cdot v'}{v^2}
\]
Substituting the known values into this formula results in:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{(1 + x^2)(1) - x(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:
\[
(1 + x^2) - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]
Therefore, the derivative simplifies to:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]
</think>

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative \( f'(x) \) is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{v(x) \cdot u'(x) - u(x) \cdot v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's apply this to our function step by step.

1. **Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u(x) = x \\
   v(x) = 1 + x^2
   \]

2. **Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):**
   \[
   u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \\
   v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(1 + x^2) = 2x
   \]

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{(1 + x^2)(1) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the numerator:**
   \[
   (1 + x^2) - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
   \]

5. **Write the final derivative:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}}

Okay, so I need to find the derivative of sec x with respect to x. Hmm, let me think about how to approach this. First, I remember that sec x is the reciprocal of cos x, right? So, sec x = 1 / cos x.

Now, to find the derivative, I can use the quotient rule because it's a function divided by another function. The quotient rule says that if you have a function f(x) divided by g(x), then the derivative is (f’(x)g(x) - f(x)g’(x)) all over [g(x)] squared.

In this case, f(x) is 1 and g(x) is cos x. So, let's find their derivatives. The derivative of f(x) which is 1 with respect to x is 0 because the slope of a constant function is zero. The derivative of g(x) = cos x is -sin x.

Putting these into the quotient rule formula: [0 * cos x - 1 * (-sin x)] / (cos x)^2. Simplifying the numerator, that becomes sin x. So the derivative is sin x over cos squared x.

Wait a minute, I think there's another way to remember this. The derivative of sec x is also known as sec x tan x because I've heard that before. Let me check if these two expressions are equivalent. 

If I have sin x / cos²x, can I express it differently? Well, since tan x = sin x / cos x and sec x = 1 / cos x, then multiplying them gives (sin x / cos x) * (1 / cos x) which is indeed sin x over cos squared x. So both forms are correct.

I think either form would be acceptable, but maybe the question expects one specific answer. Since I was taught that d/dx sec x = sec x tan x, perhaps that's the preferred form to present here.
</think>

The derivative of \(\sec(x)\) with respect to \(x\) is \(\sec(x)\tan(x)\). 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express \(\sec(x)\)** as the reciprocal of \(\cos(x)\): 
   \[
   \sec(x) = \frac{1}{\cos(x)}
   \]

2. **Apply the Quotient Rule**: The quotient rule states that the derivative of \(f/g\) is \(\frac{f'g - fg'}{g^2}\). Here, \(f = 1\) and \(g = \cos(x)\).
   
3. **Compute Derivatives**:
   - \(f'(x) = 0\)
   - \(g'(x) = -\sin(x)\)

4. **Plug into the Quotient Rule Formula**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{\cos(x)}\right) = \frac{0 \cdot \cos(x) - 1 \cdot (-\sin(x))}{\cos^2(x)} = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]

5. **Simplify Using Trigonometric Identities**:
   - Recognize that \(\sec(x) = \frac{1}{\cos(x)}\) and \(\tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}\).
   - Multiply these to get: \[
     \sec(x)\tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos^2(x)}
     \]

6. **Final Derivative**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(\sec(x)) = \sec(x)\tan(x)
   \]

**Answer:**  
The derivative is \(\boxed{\sec(x)\tan(x)}\).
